Telecommunications English

A telecommunications English curriculum for network reliability, outages, fiber and wireless deployment, service provisioning, field operations, regulatory issues, customer escalations, and technical coordination.

For network operations staff, telecom engineers, field technicians, customer operations teams, product managers, regulatory staff, and telecom project leaders.

What you will practice · Choose your pace

What you will practice

  • Give a concise incident update that separates verified impact, investigation, and a timed communication commitment.
  • Describe a construction handover accurately and request the missing acceptance evidence without overstating readiness.
  • Compare wireless observations, test a causal claim, and request measurements that distinguish coverage from capacity.
  • Resolve an incomplete service request through precise identifiers, time references, and a closed-loop read-back.
  • Elicit observable symptoms, document unknowns, and set accurate expectations for a field-service handoff.

Choose your pace

Quick practice · 15 minutes: complete one short exchange, check the explanations, and read both roles aloud.

Extended practice · two or three sittings: check the case facts, study the vocabulary and phrases, complete the longer conversation, then rehearse the follow-up exchange.

Level guide: designed for intermediate to advanced learners. B1 learners can start with the short exchanges and supplied phrases; B2 learners can complete the cloze conversations; C1 learners can focus on the distinctions in the language checks. These are teaching suggestions, not a certified level assessment.

Prompts

All operators, customers, circuits, incidents, dates, prices, contracts, and local procedures are fictional. This book teaches professional English, not network configuration, field-safety procedures, legal compliance, or emergency-call handling. Use approved operating procedures and qualified personnel. Regulatory examples identify their jurisdiction and are not complete compliance instructions.

01Network Operations and Outage BridgesThe next update is not a restoration promise+

Your goal

Give a concise incident update that separates verified impact, investigation, and a timed communication commitment.

This lesson in the book, page 4 ↗

A Read the situation

The next update is not a restoration promise

At 09:10, incident O73 interrupts 120 broadband services in the East service area. The number is a count of services, not individual people. Network alarms confirm the interruption, but the cause has not been isolated. No estimated time of restoration has been verified. Network operations lead Arun and customer communications lead Tess join an outage bridge. Their next public update is due at 09:40 Eastern. They need a consistent message without inventing a restoration time or blaming a recent change before evidence connects it to the incident.

Arun: Network operations lead / Tess: Customer communications lead

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What is the verified impact?

Answer and explanation

B. 120 broadband services in the East area

The record counts interrupted broadband services in one specified area, not people or equipment.

2. What does 09:40 Eastern represent?

Answer and explanation

C. The next public update

The team has committed to an update at that time, not to restoration.

3. What remains unconfirmed?

Answer and explanation

A. The root cause and restoration estimate

Fault isolation is ongoing and no restoration estimate has been verified.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

An affected-service count is not automatically a count of customers, households, or people. Retain the measurement unit in every update. A shared incident identifier helps different teams discuss the same event without merging unrelated reports.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
NOC
Network operations center; the team or facility monitoring network service.notify the NOC
outage bridge
A coordinated call used to manage an interruption.join the outage bridge
incident commander
The person coordinating the incident response.identify the incident commander
service impact
The effect of an incident on a defined service.confirm service impact
blast radius
Informal term for the extent of an incident's effects.assess the blast radius
fault isolation
Investigation to locate the source of a fault.continue fault isolation
alarm correlation
Analysis of related alerts to find a common pattern.perform alarm correlation
root cause
The underlying cause established through investigation.verify the root cause
ETR
Estimated time of restoration; a forecast, not automatically a guarantee.validate the ETR
restoration
Return of the affected service to its required operating state.confirm service restoration
degradation
Reduced performance without necessarily losing the entire service.report service degradation
packet loss
Failure of some transmitted data packets to reach their destination.measure packet loss
latency
Time taken for data to travel through a specified path.measure latency
jitter
Variation in packet delay over time.monitor jitter
failover
Transfer to an alternative component or path after a problem.assess failover status
redundancy
Additional capacity or components intended to improve resilience.verify redundancy
workaround
A temporary way to reduce impact without resolving the underlying cause.evaluate a workaround
change freeze
A restriction on changes during a defined period.observe the change freeze
rollback
Return to a prior configuration or release under an approved process.request a rollback
escalation path
The defined route for involving additional authority or expertise.follow the escalation path
status cadence
The agreed frequency of updates.establish a status cadence
incident timeline
A chronological record of relevant incident events.maintain the incident timeline
monitoring window
A defined period for observing service behavior.complete the monitoring window
post-incident review
A structured review of the response and underlying learning.schedule a post-incident review

An ETR is an estimate supported by the available evidence. An update time is a communication commitment. If no estimate is verified, state that clearly and still specify when the next information will be provided.

C Notice the language

Urgency needs a shared status vocabulary

On a busy bridge, short statements help only when everyone understands their status. Use confirmed, investigating, and not yet verified consistently. A direct challenge to an unsupported estimate protects the team from repeating a promise it cannot substantiate.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Open the update
Here is the confirmed service impact.
Bound the area
The interruption is limited to the East service area in our current evidence.
Retain the unit
We have identified 120 affected broadband services.
Separate cause from impact
The interruption is confirmed; the cause is still under investigation.
Reject speculation
The recent change is a lead, not a confirmed cause.
Qualify the estimate
We do not yet have a verified restoration estimate.
Name the next contact
The next public update is at 09:40 Eastern.
Prevent confusion
That is an update time, not a restoration commitment.
Ask for a source
Which monitoring result supports that statement?
Check a workaround
Has the proposed workaround been approved and validated?
Keep the bridge focused
Let us separate current impact from the investigation hypotheses.
Assign an owner
Arun will confirm the technical status before the message goes out.
Record a milestone
Please add that event and its time to the incident timeline.
Avoid premature closure
We need the agreed monitoring evidence before closing the incident.
Keep the message aligned
Customer care and the status page should use the same verified facts.
Close the handover
I will circulate the impact, open questions, owner, and next update time.
6 language and precision notes
Current evidence
Limits a statement to what has actually been established.
A lead, not
Distinguishes a useful hypothesis from a verified explanation.
Do not yet
Communicates an information gap without implying that no work is happening.
At ... Eastern
Makes the communication time unambiguous across locations.
Before closing
Names a required dependency instead of assuming the incident is resolved.
Same verified facts
Aligns messages without forcing every channel to use identical wording.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which public line is accurate?

Answer and explanation

C. We are investigating an interruption affecting 120 broadband services in the East area.

The accurate line preserves the verified service count, area, and investigation status.

2. A colleague says the recent change caused O73. What is the best response?

Answer and explanation

C. What evidence links that change to this incident?

Asking for a specific evidence link tests the claim without dismissing a potentially useful lead.

3. Which sentence correctly uses ETR?

Answer and explanation

C. No ETR has been verified; the next update is at 09:40 Eastern.

The sentence explicitly separates a restoration forecast from the communication schedule.

4. What should the handover preserve?

Answer and explanation

C. Impact, open questions, owner, and next update time

These items let the next team continue work without losing status or inventing certainty.

Check explanations in the book, page 85 ↗

E Conversations

The next update is not a restoration promise

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Arun: Network operations lead / Tess: Customer communications lead

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • Fault isolation
  • restoration
  • service impact
  • post-incident review
  • status cadence
  • incident timeline
  • monitoring window
  • affected-service count
  • incident commander
  • ETR
  1. Tess
    Care needs a line for O73 now. They are getting questions about a nationwide outage. What impact can I actually confirm, and what stays open?
  2. Arun
    Start with the 1: 120 broadband services in the East area have been interrupted since 09:10. We have not confirmed an impact outside that area.
  3. Tess
    Can I describe that as 120 customers? It sounds more natural in a public notice than a count of services.
  4. Arun
    Keep the 2. It does not tell us the number of individual people or accounts. We should not convert the figure without a reliable mapping.
  5. Tess
    Someone on the call mentioned a change made earlier this morning. Is that the explanation we should give?
  6. Arun
    No. 3 is still in progress. The change is one lead, but timing alone does not establish that it caused the interruption.
  7. Tess
    Understood. The status page has a field for the expected restoration time. Should I enter our next update time there?
  8. Arun
    Leave the 4 as unavailable under the page's approved format. Our 09:40 Eastern commitment is to publish an update, not to restore the service.
  9. Tess
    I want customers to see that we are actively working on this, even if we cannot give them a finish time.
  10. Arun
    We can say the network team is investigating. Set the 5 clearly and provide the next time. That is a concrete commitment we can keep while the technical position develops.
  11. Tess
    Can we split the bridge updates? I am hearing a proposed workaround mixed in with confirmed customer impact, and care needs to know which is which.
  12. Arun
    I will ask the 6 to separate those workstreams. A proposed workaround should not enter the customer message as available before it is approved and validated.
  13. Tess
    Who will confirm the technical wording before I send it to the status-page team and customer care?
  14. Arun
    I will. Put the current facts in the 7 as well, so later updates can show what changed and when.
  15. Tess
    If one monitoring screen turns green, can I immediately say that everyone is back online?
  16. Arun
    We need the agreed 8 and the relevant service checks. One favorable indicator alone does not demonstrate that every affected service has recovered.
  17. Tess
    Then the next message may still say we are investigating, but it will have a named owner and a clear time.
  18. Arun
    Yes. If the impact changes, describe the 9 status precisely. Do not replace an unknown cause with a guess just to make the message feel more complete.
  19. Tess
    I will keep the 120-service figure, the East area, the investigation status, and the 09:40 Eastern update commitment together.
  20. Arun
    Good. Use that same factual core across channels. After recovery, the 10 can address the fuller explanation and any follow-up actions.

Answers and explanations
  1. service impact Turn 2. Service impact names the verified effect on broadband service without treating the cause or restoration time as established.
  2. affected-service count Turn 4. Affected-service count preserves the measured unit; one customer may have several services and a service may serve several people.
  3. Fault isolation Turn 6. Fault isolation is the ongoing process of locating the fault; a recent change remains a hypothesis until evidence connects it.
  4. ETR Turn 8. ETR means estimated time of restoration; the team has no verified forecast and must not substitute a communication deadline.
  5. status cadence Turn 10. Status cadence is the agreed update frequency, giving customers a useful communication expectation without inventing technical progress.
  6. incident commander Turn 12. Incident commander identifies the person coordinating response priorities and keeping the bridge's decisions, owners, and updates aligned.
  7. incident timeline Turn 14. Incident timeline records events chronologically, preserving the source and timing of changes in the service picture.
  8. monitoring window Turn 16. Monitoring window is the defined observation period used to establish stable service rather than relying on a single favorable reading.
  9. restoration Turn 18. Restoration is the verified return of service; it should be reported from the agreed evidence, not inferred from optimism.
  10. post-incident review Turn 20. Post-incident review examines the response and causes after the incident; it is distinct from an immediate service-status update.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Tess: Care needs a line for O73 now. They are getting questions about a nationwide outage. What impact can I actually confirm, and what stays open?
  2. Arun: Start with the service impact: 120 broadband services in the East area have been interrupted since 09:10. We have not confirmed an impact outside that area.
  3. Tess: Can I describe that as 120 customers? It sounds more natural in a public notice than a count of services.
  4. Arun: Keep the affected-service count. It does not tell us the number of individual people or accounts. We should not convert the figure without a reliable mapping.
  5. Tess: Someone on the call mentioned a change made earlier this morning. Is that the explanation we should give?
  6. Arun: No. Fault isolation is still in progress. The change is one lead, but timing alone does not establish that it caused the interruption.
  7. Tess: Understood. The status page has a field for the expected restoration time. Should I enter our next update time there?
  8. Arun: Leave the ETR as unavailable under the page's approved format. Our 09:40 Eastern commitment is to publish an update, not to restore the service.
  9. Tess: I want customers to see that we are actively working on this, even if we cannot give them a finish time.
  10. Arun: We can say the network team is investigating. Set the status cadence clearly and provide the next time. That is a concrete commitment we can keep while the technical position develops.
  11. Tess: Can we split the bridge updates? I am hearing a proposed workaround mixed in with confirmed customer impact, and care needs to know which is which.
  12. Arun: I will ask the incident commander to separate those workstreams. A proposed workaround should not enter the customer message as available before it is approved and validated.
  13. Tess: Who will confirm the technical wording before I send it to the status-page team and customer care?
  14. Arun: I will. Put the current facts in the incident timeline as well, so later updates can show what changed and when.
  15. Tess: If one monitoring screen turns green, can I immediately say that everyone is back online?
  16. Arun: We need the agreed monitoring window and the relevant service checks. One favorable indicator alone does not demonstrate that every affected service has recovered.
  17. Tess: Then the next message may still say we are investigating, but it will have a named owner and a clear time.
  18. Arun: Yes. If the impact changes, describe the restoration status precisely. Do not replace an unknown cause with a guess just to make the message feel more complete.
  19. Tess: I will keep the 120-service figure, the East area, the investigation status, and the 09:40 Eastern update commitment together.
  20. Arun: Good. Use that same factual core across channels. After recovery, the post-incident review can address the fuller explanation and any follow-up actions.
Dialogue in the book, page 10 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

No verified restoration estimate

The lesson's outage bridge covers one affected service area; fault isolation continues and no restoration estimate has been verified.

  1. NOC analyst: The outage affects one service area. Fault isolation is ongoing, and we have no verified restoration estimate.
  2. Service manager: Can I tell customer support that service will probably return within the next hour?
  3. NOC analyst: We don't have evidence for that timing. Please don't turn a possible duration into an ETA.
  4. Service manager: Understood. What progress can I describe without suggesting the fault has already been found?
  5. NOC analyst: Say that the network operations center is investigating the affected area; the cause remains unconfirmed.
  6. Service manager: Should support describe other areas as unaffected, or would that extend beyond the information we have?
  7. NOC analyst: Use the confirmed scope. Don't make broader claims unless the monitoring team has checked them.
  8. Service manager: I'll separate confirmed impact, investigation status, and the missing restoration estimate in the update.
  9. NOC analyst: Good. I'll pass on verified changes through this bridge rather than speculate about the repair.
  10. Service manager: I'll make clear that the next status update is not a promise of restoration.
Alarms cleared, customer checks pending

Separate fictional case: alarms on a business-access node have cleared after a recovery action, but customer-level service checks have not finished.

  1. Incident coordinator: The node alarms have cleared after the recovery action. Customer service checks are still underway.
  2. Service assurance engineer: Then should we close the outage ticket, or does the remaining verification affect closure?
  3. Incident coordinator: It matters. Cleared alarms show one improvement, but we haven't confirmed the customer experience yet.
  4. Service assurance engineer: I'll keep the service check open and avoid describing the outage as fully resolved.
  5. Incident coordinator: Can you tell the support lead exactly what has changed since the last bridge update?
  6. Service assurance engineer: The network alarms cleared; customer-level verification remains pending. We have not established a root cause.
  7. Incident coordinator: That distinction is useful. Please don't imply that the recovery action proves what caused the fault.
  8. Service assurance engineer: Agreed. I'll record the action and observed result without presenting the sequence as causal proof.
  9. Incident coordinator: Bring the verification results back to the bridge when they are available, including any remaining failures.
  10. Service assurance engineer: I will. Closure can then be considered against the actual results and our incident process.
A monitoring gap after maintenance

Separate fictional case: after planned maintenance, traffic counters are visible but latency telemetry is missing; no customer outage has been confirmed.

  1. Shift supervisor: We can see traffic counters again, but the latency telemetry has not returned after maintenance.
  2. NOC engineer: Does that mean customers are experiencing an outage, or only that our visibility is incomplete?
  3. Shift supervisor: We have confirmed the monitoring gap, not a customer outage. Service impact still needs checking.
  4. NOC engineer: I'll describe the missing telemetry separately from the available traffic data in the handover.
  5. Shift supervisor: Please also identify which checks remain open so the next shift doesn't assume monitoring is complete.
  6. NOC engineer: The telemetry investigation and customer-impact check remain open. Neither has a confirmed completion time yet.
  7. Shift supervisor: Could the maintenance be the cause, given that the gap appeared afterward?
  8. NOC engineer: It may be related, but the timing alone doesn't establish that. We'll examine the change records.
  9. Shift supervisor: Good. Keep the status precise and avoid giving a restoration ETA for an unconfirmed service failure.
  10. NOC engineer: I'll report the monitoring recovery and service findings separately as the evidence becomes available.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

The next update is not a restoration promise
  1. Read turns 1-10; keep the service count and update time separate from people and restoration.
  2. Swap roles for turns 11-20; request the evidence needed before announcing recovery.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Separate a second update from a forecast

Incident R16 affects 45 voice services in the North area from 14:05. The next update is at 14:30 Central. The cause and restoration estimate remain unverified.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Lead: "The incident reference is ___."

Answer and explanation

A. R16

R16 is the identifier supplied for this separate interruption.

2. Agent: "The verified impact is ___."

Answer and explanation

A. 45 voice services

The record counts affected voice services, not individual users or broadband lines.

3. Lead: "Our next update is at ___."

Answer and explanation

C. 14:30 Central

This is the stated communication time and includes its time zone.

4. Agent: "The restoration estimate remains ___."

Answer and explanation

D. unverified

No verified restoration estimate is supplied, so the update time cannot replace it.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Another round with an AI partner

These prompts are fully written and include this lesson's facts and language. Open one, copy it as written, and paste it into a new AI chat.

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

Lesson 01: Network Operations and Outage Bridges
Original fictional case: At 09:10, incident O73 interrupts 120 broadband services in the East service area. The number is a count of services, not individual people. Network alarms confirm the interruption, but the cause has not been isolated. No estimated time of restoration has been verified. Network operations lead Arun and customer communications lead Tess join an outage bridge. Their next public update is due at 09:40 Eastern. They need a consistent message without inventing a restoration time or blaming a recent change before evidence connects it to the incident.
Language workshop: The next update is not a restoration promise
Communication goal: Give a concise incident update that separates verified impact, investigation, and a timed communication commitment.
Language guidance: An affected-service count is not automatically a count of customers, households, or people. Retain the measurement unit in every update. A shared incident identifier helps different teams discuss the same event without merging unrelated reports.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Here is the confirmed service impact. / The interruption is limited to the East service area in our current evidence. / We have identified 120 affected broadband services. / The interruption is confirmed; the cause is still under investigation. / The recent change is a lead, not a confirmed cause. / We do not yet have a verified restoration estimate. / The next public update is at 09:40 Eastern. / That is an update time, not a restoration commitment. / Which monitoring result supports that statement? / Has the proposed workaround been approved and validated? / Let us separate current impact from the investigation hypotheses. / Arun will confirm the technical status before the message goes out. / Please add that event and its time to the incident timeline. / We need the agreed monitoring evidence before closing the incident. / Customer care and the status page should use the same verified facts. / I will circulate the impact, open questions, owner, and next update time.
Vocabulary: NOC: Network operations center; the team or facility monitoring network service. / outage bridge: A coordinated call used to manage an interruption. / incident commander: The person coordinating the incident response. / service impact: The effect of an incident on a defined service. / blast radius: Informal term for the extent of an incident's effects. / fault isolation: Investigation to locate the source of a fault. / alarm correlation: Analysis of related alerts to find a common pattern. / root cause: The underlying cause established through investigation. / ETR: Estimated time of restoration; a forecast, not automatically a guarantee. / restoration: Return of the affected service to its required operating state. / degradation: Reduced performance without necessarily losing the entire service. / packet loss: Failure of some transmitted data packets to reach their destination. / latency: Time taken for data to travel through a specified path. / jitter: Variation in packet delay over time. / failover: Transfer to an alternative component or path after a problem. / redundancy: Additional capacity or components intended to improve resilience. / workaround: A temporary way to reduce impact without resolving the underlying cause. / change freeze: A restriction on changes during a defined period. / rollback: Return to a prior configuration or release under an approved process. / escalation path: The defined route for involving additional authority or expertise. / status cadence: The agreed frequency of updates. / incident timeline: A chronological record of relevant incident events. / monitoring window: A defined period for observing service behavior. / post-incident review: A structured review of the response and underlying learning.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-10; keep the service count and update time separate from people and restoration. Swap roles for turns 11-20; request the evidence needed before announcing recovery. Complete the transfer and retain its service type, area, and time zone.
Partner: Arun: Network operations lead / Tess: Customer communications lead
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Incident R16 affects 45 voice services in the North area from 14:05. The next update is at 14:30 Central. The cause and restoration estimate remain unverified.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Tess: Then the next message may still say we are investigating, but it will have a named owner and a clear time.
Arun: Yes. If the impact changes, describe the restoration status precisely. Do not replace an unknown cause with a guess just to make the message feel more complete.
Tess: I will keep the 120-service figure, the East area, the investigation status, and the 09:40 Eastern update commitment together.
Arun: Good. Use that same factual core across channels. After recovery, the post-incident review can address the fuller explanation and any follow-up actions.
END REFERENCE
Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Lesson 01: Network Operations and Outage Bridges
Original fictional case: At 09:10, incident O73 interrupts 120 broadband services in the East service area. The number is a count of services, not individual people. Network alarms confirm the interruption, but the cause has not been isolated. No estimated time of restoration has been verified. Network operations lead Arun and customer communications lead Tess join an outage bridge. Their next public update is due at 09:40 Eastern. They need a consistent message without inventing a restoration time or blaming a recent change before evidence connects it to the incident.
Language workshop: The next update is not a restoration promise
Communication goal: Give a concise incident update that separates verified impact, investigation, and a timed communication commitment.
Language guidance: An affected-service count is not automatically a count of customers, households, or people. Retain the measurement unit in every update. A shared incident identifier helps different teams discuss the same event without merging unrelated reports.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Here is the confirmed service impact. / The interruption is limited to the East service area in our current evidence. / We have identified 120 affected broadband services. / The interruption is confirmed; the cause is still under investigation. / The recent change is a lead, not a confirmed cause. / We do not yet have a verified restoration estimate. / The next public update is at 09:40 Eastern. / That is an update time, not a restoration commitment. / Which monitoring result supports that statement? / Has the proposed workaround been approved and validated? / Let us separate current impact from the investigation hypotheses. / Arun will confirm the technical status before the message goes out. / Please add that event and its time to the incident timeline. / We need the agreed monitoring evidence before closing the incident. / Customer care and the status page should use the same verified facts. / I will circulate the impact, open questions, owner, and next update time.
Vocabulary: NOC: Network operations center; the team or facility monitoring network service. / outage bridge: A coordinated call used to manage an interruption. / incident commander: The person coordinating the incident response. / service impact: The effect of an incident on a defined service. / blast radius: Informal term for the extent of an incident's effects. / fault isolation: Investigation to locate the source of a fault. / alarm correlation: Analysis of related alerts to find a common pattern. / root cause: The underlying cause established through investigation. / ETR: Estimated time of restoration; a forecast, not automatically a guarantee. / restoration: Return of the affected service to its required operating state. / degradation: Reduced performance without necessarily losing the entire service. / packet loss: Failure of some transmitted data packets to reach their destination. / latency: Time taken for data to travel through a specified path. / jitter: Variation in packet delay over time. / failover: Transfer to an alternative component or path after a problem. / redundancy: Additional capacity or components intended to improve resilience. / workaround: A temporary way to reduce impact without resolving the underlying cause. / change freeze: A restriction on changes during a defined period. / rollback: Return to a prior configuration or release under an approved process. / escalation path: The defined route for involving additional authority or expertise. / status cadence: The agreed frequency of updates. / incident timeline: A chronological record of relevant incident events. / monitoring window: A defined period for observing service behavior. / post-incident review: A structured review of the response and underlying learning.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-10; keep the service count and update time separate from people and restoration. Swap roles for turns 11-20; request the evidence needed before announcing recovery. Complete the transfer and retain its service type, area, and time zone.
Partner: Arun: Network operations lead / Tess: Customer communications lead
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Incident R16 affects 45 voice services in the North area from 14:05. The next update is at 14:30 Central. The cause and restoration estimate remain unverified.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Tess: Then the next message may still say we are investigating, but it will have a named owner and a clear time.
Arun: Yes. If the impact changes, describe the restoration status precisely. Do not replace an unknown cause with a guess just to make the message feel more complete.
Tess: I will keep the 120-service figure, the East area, the investigation status, and the 09:40 Eastern update commitment together.
Arun: Good. Use that same factual core across channels. After recovery, the post-incident review can address the fuller explanation and any follow-up actions.
END REFERENCE
Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

02Fiber Deployment and Construction CoordinationInstalled does not mean accepted+

Your goal

Describe a construction handover accurately and request the missing acceptance evidence without overstating readiness.

This lesson in the book, page 13 ↗

A Read the situation

Installed does not mean accepted

Fiber route F24 is physically installed. The contractor has supplied route photographs and an optical time-domain reflectometer trace, but the agreed handover package also requires an insertion-loss report, updated route drawings, and an inspection sign-off. Those three items remain pending. The next team has scheduled service work on the assumption that the route is accepted. Deployment coordinator Hana and construction lead Victor must correct that assumption. No inspection failure has been reported, but neither installation photographs nor a single test trace establish completion of the agreed acceptance process.

Hana: Deployment coordinator / Victor: Construction lead

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. Which milestone is complete?

Answer and explanation

B. Physical installation of route F24

Physical installation is confirmed, while the required acceptance items remain pending.

2. Which item has been supplied?

Answer and explanation

C. An OTDR trace

The contractor supplied the trace and photographs, not the three outstanding items.

3. What can be said about inspection?

Answer and explanation

A. Sign-off is pending; no failure is reported.

Pending sign-off establishes neither a successful inspection nor a reported inspection failure.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

An OTDR trace helps locate and characterize events along a fiber. It is not a substitute for every required measurement. Here the agreed insertion-loss report remains a separate handover requirement, regardless of how convincing the trace appears.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
fiber route
The path followed by an installed fiber cable system.identify the fiber route
cable plant
The installed cabling and associated connecting components.document the cable plant
as-built drawing
A drawing showing the installation as actually constructed.update the as-built drawing
splice enclosure
A protective housing for joined fiber sections.identify the splice enclosure
splice schedule
A record of planned or completed fiber connections.reconcile the splice schedule
fiber strand
An individual optical fiber within a cable.identify the fiber strand
termination
The prepared end connection of a cable or fiber.document the termination
patch panel
A panel organizing connections between cables and equipment.label the patch panel
OTDR
Optical time-domain reflectometer; an instrument characterizing fiber events using backscattered light and reflections.review the OTDR trace
insertion loss
Reduction in optical power through a link or component.record insertion loss
loss budget
The planned allowance for optical loss across a link.check the loss budget
acceptance criterion
A stated condition that must be met for acceptance.confirm the acceptance criteria
test report
A documented account of test methods and results.submit the test report
inspection sign-off
Recorded approval following the specified inspection.obtain inspection sign-off
handover package
The agreed documents and evidence supplied at transfer.complete the handover package
punch list
A recorded list of remaining defects or unfinished items.close the punch list
snag
A defect or incomplete item requiring attention.record a snag
demarcation point
The defined boundary between service or ownership responsibilities.identify the demarcation point
wayleave
Permission to install or maintain infrastructure across another party's land.check the wayleave
right of way
A legal or designated right to use a route or corridor.confirm the right of way
duct
A protective conduit through which cable is routed.identify the duct
pull section
A defined route segment used in cable-installation planning.document the pull section
reinstatement
Restoration of disturbed surfaces after construction work.inspect the reinstatement
ready for service
A status indicating the specified service-readiness conditions are met.verify ready-for-service status

Pending means not yet completed or decided. It does not mean failed. Use physically installed, inspection pending, and acceptance pending separately so that a planning team does not infer service readiness from a narrower construction milestone.

C Notice the language

Milestone words carry operational consequences

One team may use done to mean its own physical task has ended, while another hears permission to proceed. Use the named milestone and list the remaining dependencies. Ask for an owner and evidence instead of assigning blame for the misunderstanding.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Name the completed task
Physical installation of F24 is complete.
Correct the status
The route has not yet been accepted.
List the supplied evidence
We have photographs and an OTDR trace.
Identify a missing item
The insertion-loss report is still outstanding.
Request the current drawing
Please supply the updated as-built route drawing.
Clarify the inspection
Inspection sign-off is pending; no failure has been reported.
Separate the measurements
The trace does not replace the agreed loss report.
Refer to the agreement
Let us check the handover package against the acceptance criteria.
Ask about ownership
Who is responsible for each outstanding item?
Avoid a date assumption
Do we have confirmed submission dates or only targets?
Qualify downstream planning
The service work depends on acceptance, not just installation.
Correct the schedule note
Please mark the route as installed, acceptance pending.
Check the boundary
Which demarcation point does this report cover?
Keep evidence traceable
The test result needs the route and fiber identifiers.
Escalate the dependency
I will flag the acceptance gap to the scheduling team.
Close the handover
We will confirm readiness after the required evidence is reviewed.
6 language and precision notes
Has not yet
Describes an incomplete milestone without implying permanent failure.
Still outstanding
Identifies a required item that has not been supplied.
Does not replace
Prevents one type of evidence from standing in for a different requirement.
Against the criteria
Gives the review a defined reference instead of a general impression.
Depends on
Makes the sequence between milestones explicit.
Targets or confirmed dates
Distinguishes planning intentions from reliable commitments.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which schedule note is supported?

Answer and explanation

D. F24 installed; acceptance pending.

The note states the completed physical work while preserving the unresolved acceptance status.

2. What should Hana request?

Answer and explanation

C. The missing reports, drawings, and inspection sign-off with owners and dates

The request addresses the three actual dependencies and makes follow-up responsibility clear.

3. Which sentence handles the inspection accurately?

Answer and explanation

B. We are awaiting sign-off; no failure has been reported.

The evidence supports pending approval but contains no report of an inspection failure.

4. What is the best response to the OTDR argument?

Answer and explanation

C. The trace is useful, but the agreed insertion-loss report is still required.

The response recognizes the supplied evidence without replacing a distinct agreed acceptance requirement.

E Conversations

Installed does not mean accepted

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Hana: Deployment coordinator / Victor: Construction lead

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • fiber route
  • handover package
  • OTDR
  • demarcation point
  • fiber strand
  • Insertion loss
  • acceptance criteria
  • inspection sign-off
  • as-built drawing
  • ready for service
  1. Hana
    F24 is on tomorrow's service schedule. The note just says complete. Does that mean construction has finished, or has the route actually been accepted?
  2. Victor
    The 1 is physically installed. That is what the contractor reported, but I have not issued an acceptance notice for the handover.
  3. Hana
    We need to correct the schedule before that wording travels further. What evidence has actually arrived?
  4. Victor
    We have photographs and an 2 trace. The agreed package also includes an insertion-loss report, updated route drawings, and inspection sign-off. Those three items are still outstanding.
  5. Hana
    The contractor says the trace looks good. Does that make the separate loss report a paperwork formality?
  6. Victor
    No. 3 is a separate required result in our agreement. We should not treat one type of evidence as a replacement for another because it looks reassuring.
  7. Hana
    The current route drawing is the design version. Does the handover need to show the final installed path?
  8. Victor
    Yes, we need the 4 for the completed installation. If the route or connections changed, the receiving team needs the actual arrangement, not an assumption that the original plan stayed unchanged.
  9. Hana
    Is the inspection delayed because something failed, or do we simply not have a recorded decision yet?
  10. Victor
    We are awaiting 5. No failure has been reported. I would avoid saying passed or failed until we have the inspection record and its actual outcome.
  11. Hana
    Then I should tell the service team that installation is complete but acceptance remains pending.
  12. Victor
    Exactly. Ask them to plan against the 6, not the contractor's shorthand. We need the outstanding evidence reviewed before anyone turns that status into ready for service.
  13. Hana
    Please send an owner and expected date for each missing item. Which dates are firm, and which are still targets?
  14. Victor
    I will update the 7 tracker with owners and target dates. I will mark a date confirmed only when the responsible person has actually committed to it.
  15. Hana
    The test files also need to identify what was measured. There are several fibers and more than one termination point.
  16. Victor
    Agreed. Each result needs the route, 8, and endpoint references. A technically valid result is still unhelpful if the receiving team cannot match it to the connection they will use.
  17. Hana
    I will move the schedule note out of the accepted column and explain the dependency to the next team.
  18. Victor
    Use 9 only after the required review. That keeps the milestone meaningful and avoids asking the next team to discover the missing records on site.
  19. Hana
    I will also keep pending separate from failed. We need to correct the assumption without inventing a construction defect.
  20. Victor
    Thank you. Once the evidence is complete, we can confirm the 10 and the accepted scope together, then notify scheduling with a clear, traceable status.

Answers and explanations
  1. fiber route Turn 2. Fiber route identifies the specific installed cable path; completing its physical work does not establish every acceptance milestone.
  2. OTDR Turn 4. OTDR identifies the instrument used to produce the supplied trace; that trace does not satisfy every separate handover requirement.
  3. Insertion loss Turn 6. Insertion loss describes optical power reduction through the link; its required report is distinct from the supplied event trace.
  4. as-built drawing Turn 8. As-built drawing records the actual constructed arrangement rather than simply repeating the planned design.
  5. inspection sign-off Turn 10. Inspection sign-off is the recorded approval still pending; its absence does not establish that the inspection failed.
  6. acceptance criteria Turn 12. Acceptance criteria are the agreed conditions for accepting the route, rather than a general impression that construction looks finished.
  7. handover package Turn 14. Handover package brings together the agreed documents and evidence; tracking its missing items makes the transfer dependency explicit.
  8. fiber strand Turn 16. Fiber strand identifies the individual optical fiber tested, preventing a result for one strand from being assumed to cover another.
  9. ready for service Turn 18. Ready for service is a defined readiness status; it should follow the required evidence and authorization, not physical installation alone.
  10. demarcation point Turn 20. Demarcation point defines the responsibility boundary at handover, helping the receiving team understand exactly what is being transferred.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Hana: F24 is on tomorrow's service schedule. The note just says complete. Does that mean construction has finished, or has the route actually been accepted?
  2. Victor: The fiber route is physically installed. That is what the contractor reported, but I have not issued an acceptance notice for the handover.

Dialogue in the book, page 19 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Physical completion is not inspection clearance

The lesson's fiber installation is physically complete but inspection remains pending; the receiving team assumes the route is ready.

  1. Construction coordinator: The physical fiber work is complete, but inspection is still pending. I'm handing over an incomplete readiness status.
  2. Activation supervisor: Our team thought the route was ready. Are you asking us to begin service activation?
  3. Construction coordinator: No. I'm asking you to acknowledge the physical completion and the outstanding inspection separately.
  4. Activation supervisor: Understood. Who will provide the inspection result so we know when to reconsider the next stage?
  5. Construction coordinator: I'll coordinate that update with the inspection owner. I don't yet have a confirmed result or timing.
  6. Activation supervisor: Should we also verify the handover records for splicing and any relevant permit conditions?
  7. Construction coordinator: Yes. Use the actual records rather than inferring their status from physical completion.
  8. Activation supervisor: I'll keep the receiving task pending and tell my team that route readiness is not confirmed.
  9. Construction coordinator: Please repeat the main dependency when you brief them, so the distinction survives the handoff.
  10. Activation supervisor: Physical work is complete; inspection and readiness verification remain outstanding. We await your evidence-based update.
Splice records and the wrong route label

Separate fictional case: a fiber handover package names route F12, while its splice diagram is labeled F21; neither team knows which label is correct.

  1. Field coordinator: This handover names route F12, but the attached splice diagram says F21. The route identity needs checking.
  2. Network records specialist: Could that be a typing error, or are we looking at two different fiber routes?
  3. Field coordinator: Either is possible. I can't confirm which, so please don't relabel the diagram on an assumption.
  4. Network records specialist: I'll hold the records update and compare the source package with the project references.
  5. Field coordinator: I'll contact the construction lead for the verified route identifier and the corresponding splicing record.
  6. Network records specialist: What should I tell the provisioning team if they ask whether the documentation is ready?
  7. Field coordinator: Say the package was received but contains an unresolved identity mismatch. Receipt doesn't mean acceptance.
  8. Network records specialist: Understood. My task is document verification, and yours is obtaining confirmation from construction.
  9. Field coordinator: Correct. Once we reconcile the identifiers, I'll send a clearly versioned replacement handover package.
  10. Network records specialist: I'll acknowledge the corrected package and confirm which records have actually been updated.
Make-ready ownership at a shared site

Separate fictional case: a project uses make-ready to mean site preparation. The civil contractor has completed the duct inspection; the building-access confirmation remains with the property team.

  1. Civil works coordinator: The duct inspection is complete. Building access remains unconfirmed, and the property team owns that check.
  2. Fiber deployment lead: Our schedule says make-ready complete. Does that label include building access in this project?
  3. Civil works coordinator: It should not imply that all preparation is finished. Only the duct inspection is complete here.
  4. Fiber deployment lead: I'll correct our readiness summary before the installation crew receives its assignment.
  5. Civil works coordinator: Please carry forward the access dependency and the property team's responsibility in your handover.
  6. Fiber deployment lead: Can you transfer the completed inspection record while I contact the property coordinator?
  7. Civil works coordinator: Yes. I'll send the identified record, but I cannot authorize site entry on their behalf.
  8. Fiber deployment lead: Understood. Receiving the inspection record doesn't give the crew permission to enter the building.
  9. Civil works coordinator: Exactly. Let me know whether the property team accepts ownership of the access confirmation.
  10. Fiber deployment lead: I'll obtain that acknowledgment and keep crew scheduling conditional on the remaining project checks.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

Installed does not mean accepted
  1. Read turns 1-10; name the supplied evidence and the three outstanding items.
  2. Swap roles for turns 11-20; distinguish installation, inspection, and acceptance status.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Check a second handover

Route C18 is installed. Its drawings and loss report are supplied, but inspection sign-off is pending. The agreed local process requires that sign-off before acceptance.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Coordinator: "The route identifier is ___."

Answer and explanation

C. C18

C18 is the route named in this separate handover record.

2. Contractor: "The completed milestone is ___."

Answer and explanation

B. physical installation

The record confirms installation, not completion of every acceptance requirement.

3. Coordinator: "The outstanding approval is ___."

Answer and explanation

C. inspection sign-off

Drawings and loss results are supplied, leaving the stated inspection approval pending.

4. Contractor: "The acceptance status remains ___."

Answer and explanation

B. pending

The local acceptance condition has not been met while the required sign-off remains outstanding.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Lesson 02: Fiber Deployment and Construction Coordination
Original fictional case: Fiber route F24 is physically installed. The contractor has supplied route photographs and an optical time-domain reflectometer trace, but the agreed handover package also requires an insertion-loss report, updated route drawings, and an inspection sign-off. Those three items remain pending. The next team has scheduled service work on the assumption that the route is accepted. Deployment coordinator Hana and construction lead Victor must correct that assumption. No inspection failure has been reported, but neither installation photographs nor a single test trace establish completion of the agreed acceptance process.
Language workshop: Installed does not mean accepted
Communication goal: Describe a construction handover accurately and request the missing acceptance evidence without overstating readiness.
Language guidance: An OTDR trace helps locate and characterize events along a fiber. It is not a substitute for every required measurement. Here the agreed insertion-loss report remains a separate handover requirement, regardless of how convincing the trace appears.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Physical installation of F24 is complete. / The route has not yet been accepted. / We have photographs and an OTDR trace. / The insertion-loss report is still outstanding. / Please supply the updated as-built route drawing. / Inspection sign-off is pending; no failure has been reported. / The trace does not replace the agreed loss report. / Let us check the handover package against the acceptance criteria. / Who is responsible for each outstanding item? / Do we have confirmed submission dates or only targets? / The service work depends on acceptance, not just installation. / Please mark the route as installed, acceptance pending. / Which demarcation point does this report cover? / The test result needs the route and fiber identifiers. / I will flag the acceptance gap to the scheduling team. / We will confirm readiness after the required evidence is reviewed.
Vocabulary: fiber route: The path followed by an installed fiber cable system. / cable plant: The installed cabling and associated connecting components. / as-built drawing: A drawing showing the installation as actually constructed. / splice enclosure: A protective housing for joined fiber sections. / splice schedule: A record of planned or completed fiber connections. / fiber strand: An individual optical fiber within a cable. / termination: The prepared end connection of a cable or fiber. / patch panel: A panel organizing connections between cables and equipment. / OTDR: Optical time-domain reflectometer; an instrument characterizing fiber events using backscattered light and reflections. / insertion loss: Reduction in optical power through a link or component. / loss budget: The planned allowance for optical loss across a link. / acceptance criterion: A stated condition that must be met for acceptance. / test report: A documented account of test methods and results. / inspection sign-off: Recorded approval following the specified inspection. / handover package: The agreed documents and evidence supplied at transfer. / punch list: A recorded list of remaining defects or unfinished items. / snag: A defect or incomplete item requiring attention. / demarcation point: The defined boundary between service or ownership responsibilities. / wayleave: Permission to install or maintain infrastructure across another party's land. / right of way: A legal or designated right to use a route or corridor. / duct: A protective conduit through which cable is routed. / pull section: A defined route segment used in cable-installation planning. / reinstatement: Restoration of disturbed surfaces after construction work. / ready for service: A status indicating the specified service-readiness conditions are met.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-10; name the supplied evidence and the three outstanding items. Swap roles for turns 11-20; distinguish installation, inspection, and acceptance status. Complete the transfer without converting pending sign-off into a failed inspection.
Partner: Hana: Deployment coordinator / Victor: Construction lead
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Route C18 is installed. Its drawings and loss report are supplied, but inspection sign-off is pending. The agreed local process requires that sign-off before acceptance.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Hana: I will move the schedule note out of the accepted column and explain the dependency to the next team.
Victor: Use ready for service only after the required review. That keeps the milestone meaningful and avoids asking the next team to discover the missing records on site.
Hana: I will also keep pending separate from failed. We need to correct the assumption without inventing a construction defect.
Victor: Thank you. Once the evidence is complete, we can confirm the demarcation point and the accepted scope together, then notify scheduling with a clear, traceable status.
END REFERENCE

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.
