Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Turn internet bad into a usable dispatch request

The lesson's field ticket says only internet bad; the customer is available for a clarification call.

  1. Field technician: Could you arrange a customer clarification call? The ticket says internet bad but doesn't describe the symptoms.
  2. Dispatch coordinator: What information would help you prepare without asking the customer to troubleshoot unfamiliar equipment?
  3. Field technician: Please ask what stops working, when it happens, and whether it affects one device or several.
  4. Dispatch coordinator: Should I ask them to inspect inside wiring before we schedule a truck roll?
  5. Field technician: No. Start with their observations and follow our approved support process for any additional checks.
  6. Dispatch coordinator: They are available now. I can ask those questions and capture their answers in the ticket.
  7. Field technician: Thank you. Include their availability for a visit, but don't promise a repair time or diagnosis.
  8. Dispatch coordinator: Would you like me to request the existing signal-level records from service support as well?
  9. Field technician: Yes, if available through the normal channel. That would help distinguish measured information from reported symptoms.
  10. Dispatch coordinator: I'll collect the observations and available records, then confirm what dispatch can arrange.
Site access before a truck roll

Separate fictional case: a technician is assigned to a locked equipment room, but the dispatch ticket has no authorized site contact.

  1. Field supervisor: Please obtain an authorized site contact before confirming this truck roll to the locked equipment room.
  2. Dispatch planner: The address is complete. Why can't the technician simply ask reception for access on arrival?
  3. Field supervisor: We haven't established that reception can authorize access. The visit needs a confirmed contact and arrangement.
  4. Dispatch planner: I'll ask the customer's facilities coordinator who can meet the technician and arrange the approved access.
  5. Field supervisor: Please include the person's work contact details through the ticket system, not in a public chat.
  6. Dispatch planner: Should I keep the proposed visit time or offer a different slot while I ask?
  7. Field supervisor: Ask whether the proposed time works. Don't describe it as confirmed until the required coordination is complete.
  8. Dispatch planner: Understood. This request concerns access readiness, not permission to bypass the locked room's controls.
  9. Field supervisor: Exactly. A useful dispatch request includes what the technician needs and why it matters.
  10. Dispatch planner: I'll obtain the access arrangement and report any remaining obstacle before confirming the visit.
A repeat visit needs the earlier evidence

Separate fictional case: a second technician is assigned to intermittent connection drops; the first visit's signal readings are mentioned in a ticket but not attached.

  1. Field technician: Could you retrieve the first visit's signal readings before we prepare this repeat service visit?
  2. Service coordinator: The ticket says readings taken, but it doesn't include the values or measurement conditions.
  3. Field technician: Please ask the original technician for the recorded results, including the time and measurement location.
  4. Service coordinator: Would it be enough to summarize the visit as signal normal? That's what the customer remembers.
  5. Field technician: That is a reported recollection, not the measurement record. We need to preserve the distinction.
  6. Service coordinator: I'll request the actual record and note that the customer's account is separate evidence.
  7. Field technician: Also ask whether any inside-wiring observations were recorded, without implying they explain the current drops.
  8. Service coordinator: I can request both sets of notes. I can't promise the original technician will respond before dispatch.
  9. Field technician: Then tell dispatch what remains missing, so the next visit isn't planned around assumed findings.
  10. Service coordinator: I'll send the available evidence and a clear list of outstanding questions to the receiving technician.

Two scenarios to practice

  1. Read turns 1-10; ask for observable symptoms rather than a guessed diagnosis.
  2. Swap roles for turns 11-20; distinguish the evening symptom window from Friday access.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Prepare a second dispatch summary

Ticket P28 reports voice-call dropouts on one wired desk phone from 10:00 to 11:00. Other phones have not been checked. The customer offers Monday 15:00-17:00 for access; no booking exists.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Dispatcher: "The affected device is ___."

Answer and explanation

C. one wired desk phone

The report identifies one wired phone, not every phone or a wireless laptop.

2. Customer: "The reported symptom window is ___."

Answer and explanation

A. 10:00-11:00

This is when the dropouts occur, not the offered access period.

3. Dispatcher: "Other-phone performance is ___."

Answer and explanation

D. not checked

No observations for other phones are supplied, so their performance remains unverified.

4. Customer: "My access availability is ___."

Answer and explanation

C. Monday 15:00-17:00

This is offered availability only; the separate record says no appointment has been booked.

Speaking task: Read turns 1-10; ask for observable symptoms rather than a guessed diagnosis. Swap roles for turns 11-20; distinguish the evening symptom window from Friday access. Complete the transfer and keep the unchecked phones separate from the reported device.
Partner: Paulo: Service dispatcher / Erin: Customer office coordinator
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.

Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Paulo: Correct. The next step depends on the approved diagnostic process and what the investigation finds. I cannot promise a repair result now.
Erin: Then include the evening pattern in the work order if dispatch proceeds. Otherwise the technician may arrive at a time when the symptom is not visible.
Paulo: I will pass on the two laptops, Wi-Fi connection, evening dropouts, unknown wired status, and your access availability. Do not send passwords in the ticket.
Erin: Thank you. That should make the dispatch more useful. Please send the booking decision separately so our staff do not mistake this clarification call for a confirmed visit.

Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Lesson 05: Field Service and Dispatch

Erin can provide access from 13:00 to 15:00 on Friday, but that availability has not been booked. A visit, if confirmed, would be an investigation appointment rather than a guarantee of repair within the window.

Language guidance: Customer-reported identifies the source of an observation, not a reason to dismiss it. Record the application, devices, connection type, and time pattern. Do not widen two Wi-Fi laptops into every device or turn unknown wired performance into a confirmed fault.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

06Regulatory and Emergency ServicesAlways works is not an approved notice+

Your goal

Challenge an unsupported service claim and coordinate a clear, service-specific customer notice with the responsible reviewers.

This lesson in the book, page 49 ↗

A Read the situation

Always works is not an approved notice

A U.S. interconnected Voice over Internet Protocol provider is preparing a customer notice. The draft says, "911 always works, even without power or broadband." Product records identify dependence on powered customer equipment and a broadband connection, but the draft has not been reviewed against the current applicable requirements or approved service wording. Communications editor Celia and compliance reviewer Marcus must remove the absolute assurance, obtain verified service-specific limitations, and route the notice for review. The task is not to invent a universal disclaimer or conduct an emergency test call.

Celia: Customer communications editor / Marcus: Compliance reviewer

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What is wrong with the draft assurance?

Answer and explanation

C. It promises availability despite dependencies identified in the product record.

The absolute assurance conflicts with known equipment and broadband dependencies and lacks the required review.

2. What should happen next?

Answer and explanation

D. Verify the limitations and route revised wording through the responsible review process.

The task requires verified service-specific content and review, not unsupported publication or an emergency test call.

3. What jurisdiction and service context are stated?

Answer and explanation

C. U.S. interconnected VoIP

The brief expressly identifies the U.S. interconnected-VoIP context, which limits the regulatory discussion.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

VoIP describes a technology family; interconnected VoIP is a specific U.S. regulatory category. Emergency-calling obligations and location arrangements depend on the service and applicable rules. Do not turn one product example into a universal statement about all calling services.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
VoIP
Voice over Internet Protocol; voice communication carried over IP networks.describe the VoIP service
interconnected VoIP
A regulatory service category connecting IP-based voice with the public telephone network.identify interconnected-VoIP obligations
E911
Enhanced 911; emergency calling with associated routing and caller information functions.review E911 requirements
PSAP
Public safety answering point; a facility receiving emergency calls.identify the relevant PSAP
dispatchable location
Location information sufficient to identify where assistance is needed under applicable rules.verify dispatchable-location requirements
registered location
Location information supplied or maintained for a service under applicable arrangements.update the registered location
fixed service
A service associated with a fixed location under the relevant definition.determine fixed-service requirements
non-fixed service
A service not restricted to a fixed location under the relevant definition.review non-fixed-service requirements
nomadic use
Use of a service from changing locations.assess nomadic-use implications
power dependency
Reliance on electrical power for service operation.disclose the power dependency
broadband dependency
Reliance on a broadband connection for service operation.explain the broadband dependency
backup power
An alternative power source intended to support operation during a primary-power loss.verify backup-power capabilities
service limitation
A condition restricting availability, function, or performance.explain a service limitation
customer notification
Information provided to customers about a relevant service matter.review the customer notification
affirmative acknowledgment
Active confirmation of specified information; this advisory context requires receipt and understanding.record affirmative acknowledgment
prominence
The degree to which important information is noticeable.assess the notice's prominence
plain-language notice
A notice using understandable wording while preserving necessary facts.prepare a plain-language notice
absolute assurance
An unconditional promise such as always or never.remove an absolute assurance
applicability
Whether a requirement covers the specific service or situation.assess applicability
approved wording
Language authorized through the responsible review process.use approved wording
version control
Identification and management of changes to a document or record.maintain version control
compliance review
Assessment against applicable requirements by responsible personnel.request compliance review
publication hold
A restriction preventing release until specified conditions are met.maintain the publication hold
review record
Documentation of who reviewed which version and the result.retain the review record

Backup power is not an unlimited guarantee, and local equipment power is only one possible dependency. State verified limitations in clear, prominent language and obtain the appropriate review. This exercise does not provide a complete customer-notification template.

C Notice the language

Plain language can still preserve conditions

Removing jargon should make a limitation easier to understand, not make it disappear. Explain the customer consequence, retain the relevant conditions, and ask the accountable team to verify the wording. A confident tone does not justify an absolute service guarantee.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Identify the claim
The draft says that 911 always works.
State the conflict
That assurance is not supported by the product record.
Name the dependencies
The service depends on powered equipment and broadband.
Limit the context
We are reviewing a U.S. interconnected-VoIP service.
Ask for current requirements
Which current requirements apply to this specific service?
Request verified wording
Please confirm the service-specific limitations.
Avoid a universal disclaimer
We should not reuse a generic notice without checking applicability.
Keep the consequence clear
The customer needs to understand when calling may be limited or unavailable.
Retain prominence
The limitation should not disappear into fine print.
Separate review from release
A revised draft is not yet approved for publication.
Check the version
Which document version has the reviewer cleared?
Record the decision
Please retain the review outcome with the approved text.
Question the absolute
Can we substantiate always under the stated conditions?
Protect emergency resources
We are not making an emergency call to test this wording.
Assign the review
Product and compliance need to verify the technical facts and applicable language.
Close the release condition
Keep the notice on hold until the responsible review is complete.
6 language and precision notes
Not supported by
Challenges the evidence behind a claim rather than the writer personally.
This specific service
Prevents an example from becoming a universal regulatory statement.
May be limited or unavailable
Describes a possible service consequence without promising a uniform outcome.
Not yet approved
Distinguishes editing progress from release authorization.
Which version
Makes approval traceable to an exact document.
Until ... complete
States the condition for ending the publication hold.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which revision approach is appropriate?

Answer and explanation

A. Replace the absolute with verified service-specific limitations and obtain review.

The appropriate approach preserves verified facts, relevance, readability, and the required review.

2. Which statement should be challenged?

Answer and explanation

C. Backup power means emergency calling can never fail.

Backup power alone does not establish an unconditional guarantee across all equipment and network conditions.

3. What does an approved wording record need?

Answer and explanation

D. The exact version and documented review outcome

Version-specific records distinguish actual authorization from an informal or unrelated review.

4. What does this scenario authorize?

Answer and explanation

A. Review of customer-notice language, not an emergency test call

The exercise concerns communication review and explicitly excludes using emergency calling as a test.

E Conversations

Always works is not an approved notice

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Celia: Customer communications editor / Marcus: Compliance reviewer

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank

  • backup power

  • plain-language notice
  • service limitation

  • prominence
  • approved wording

  1. Celia
    Can we hold this notice? The headline promises 911 always works without power or broadband, but the product notes list both as dependencies.
  2. Marcus
    It is an 1 that our product record does not support. We need to stop that version from being published while we verify the relevant service facts.
  3. Celia
    The product notes say the customer equipment requires power and the voice service uses a broadband connection. Neither dependency appears in the draft.
  4. Marcus
    Then each 2 needs to be explained accurately. Reassurance should come from useful information, not from deleting conditions that may affect whether the service works.
  5. Celia
    Can I take a standard paragraph from another provider and replace the product name? That would be quicker than starting again.
  6. Marcus
    First check 3. We are dealing with a U.S. interconnected-VoIP service, and the actual product, location arrangements, and current requirements matter. Another service's paragraph may not fit.
  7. Celia
    I want the revised notice to be understandable without asking customers to decode technical language.
  8. Marcus
    A 4 can say what customers need to know about possible limitations. Product staff must verify the facts, and the responsible reviewers must check the resulting wording.
  9. Celia
    Should we say that a battery solves the problem? Some equipment has backup-power options.
  10. Marcus
    Do not infer a guarantee from 5. We need verified capabilities and conditions. Power at one device does not automatically establish that every part of the calling path remains available.
  11. Celia
    The limitation is in a small note below the reassurance. Please review the layout too; changing the paragraph will not help if the headline still contradicts it.
  12. Marcus
    We need to assess 6 as well as wording. A clear sentence loses value if the layout makes it easy to miss or the headline contradicts it.
  13. Celia
    I will remove the always statement, list the questions for product, and send the replacement draft through the review process.
  14. Marcus
    Keep the 7 in place. A better draft is progress, but it is not the same thing as a document cleared for customer release.
  15. Celia
    We have two versions circulating. One has the old headline and the other has my changes. That could become confusing.
  16. Marcus
    Use 8 and retire the old release copy from the active workflow. The review should point to an exact version, not a vague reference to the latest draft.
  17. Celia
    I will ask product to verify the limitations and compliance to identify the current requirements, including the relevant customer-notification process.
  18. Marcus
    Yes. Retain the 9. Do not describe our edited paragraph as a complete compliance solution before those checks and any associated process requirements are addressed.
  19. Celia
    And we are reviewing language, not asking staff to call emergency services to see what happens.
  20. Marcus
    Correct. Once the responsible process is complete, use the 10 for this service. Keep the customer consequence clear and do not restore an unsupported promise for a more reassuring tone.

Answers and explanations
  1. absolute assurance Turn 2. Absolute assurance is an unconditional promise unsupported by the documented equipment and broadband dependencies.
  2. service limitation Turn 4. Service limitation identifies a condition affecting availability; customers need that condition to understand the service.
  3. applicability Turn 6. Applicability asks whether the requirement and wording fit this particular service, jurisdiction, and operating arrangement.
  4. plain-language notice Turn 8. Plain-language notice makes the facts understandable while preserving the relevant dependencies and consequences.
  5. backup power Turn 10. Backup power is an alternative power source; its existence alone does not guarantee the entire service path remains available.
  6. prominence Turn 12. Prominence concerns whether customers notice the limitation; a contradictory headline can undermine the notice.
  7. publication hold Turn 14. Publication hold prevents the unreviewed notice from being released while technical facts and applicable requirements are checked.
  8. version control Turn 16. Version control identifies the reviewed document and prevents an obsolete draft from being mistaken for approved text.
  9. review record Turn 18. Review record documents the version, responsible reviewers, and outcome, preserving evidence of what was actually checked and approved.
  10. approved wording Turn 20. Approved wording is language authorized for the identified service and version after the required review, not merely a plausible draft.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Celia: Can we hold this notice? The headline promises 911 always works without power or broadband, but the product notes list both as dependencies.
  2. Marcus: It is an absolute assurance that our product record does not support. We need to stop that version from being published while we verify the relevant service facts.
  3. Celia: The product notes say the customer equipment requires power and the voice service uses a broadband connection. Neither dependency appears in the draft.
  4. Marcus: Then each service limitation needs to be explained accurately. Reassurance should come from useful information, not from deleting conditions that may affect whether the service works.
  5. Celia: Can I take a standard paragraph from another provider and replace the product name? That would be quicker than starting again.
  6. Marcus: First check applicability. We are dealing with a U.S. interconnected-VoIP service, and the actual product, location arrangements, and current requirements matter. Another service's paragraph may not fit.
  7. Celia: I want the revised notice to be understandable without asking customers to decode technical language.
  8. Marcus: A plain-language notice can say what customers need to know about possible limitations. Product staff must verify the facts, and the responsible reviewers must check the resulting wording.
  9. Celia: Should we say that a battery solves the problem? Some equipment has backup-power options.
  10. Marcus: Do not infer a guarantee from backup power. We need verified capabilities and conditions. Power at one device does not automatically establish that every part of the calling path remains available.
  11. Celia: The limitation is in a small note below the reassurance. Please review the layout too; changing the paragraph will not help if the headline still contradicts it.
  12. Marcus: We need to assess prominence as well as wording. A clear sentence loses value if the layout makes it easy to miss or the headline contradicts it.
  13. Celia: I will remove the always statement, list the questions for product, and send the replacement draft through the review process.
  14. Marcus: Keep the publication hold in place. A better draft is progress, but it is not the same thing as a document cleared for customer release.
  15. Celia: We have two versions circulating. One has the old headline and the other has my changes. That could become confusing.

Dialogue in the book, page 55 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Unreviewed wording in a customer notice

The lesson's customer notice describes a regulatory requirement without current approved wording or review by the responsible team.

  1. Customer communications editor: This customer notice describes a regulatory requirement, but I can't find the current approved wording.
  2. Regulatory coordinator: Then we shouldn't present the statement as verified. The responsible team hasn't reviewed this draft yet.
  3. Customer communications editor: Could I keep the wording and add that requirements may vary, as a temporary solution?
  4. Regulatory coordinator: A general caveat doesn't establish that the specific claim is correct. We need the appropriate review.
  5. Customer communications editor: I'll mark that section pending and ask for the source and applicable customer scope.
  6. Regulatory coordinator: Good. Keep any E911 or emergency-service description tied to the reviewed service and context.
  7. Customer communications editor: So I should not borrow language from another notice just because the service names look similar?
  8. Regulatory coordinator: Correct. We haven't confirmed that the wording applies here or that the older notice is current.
  9. Customer communications editor: I'll route the draft with the unresolved claims identified rather than publish an unsupported explanation.
  10. Regulatory coordinator: I'll obtain the responsible team's response and distinguish approved wording from questions still under review.
A filed document is not a confirmed approval

Separate fictional case: a project tracker shows a regulatory filing submitted; no response from the reviewing body has been recorded.

  1. Program manager: The regulatory filing is submitted. Can the status slide say approval complete for the launch meeting?
  2. Regulatory affairs specialist: No. Submission is recorded, but we have no recorded response from the reviewing body.
  3. Program manager: I thought filing meant that the necessary regulatory step had been accepted.
  4. Regulatory affairs specialist: Here, it means the document was sent. We must check the actual process before claiming acceptance or approval.
  5. Program manager: Then I'll state submitted and leave the review outcome unresolved in the project status.
  6. Regulatory affairs specialist: Please also avoid assuming that submission alone establishes permission for any customer-facing change.
  7. Program manager: Could you identify which confirmation the project needs next, using the applicable internal guidance?
  8. Regulatory affairs specialist: Yes. I'll check with the responsible reviewer and report the required next step.
  9. Program manager: That gives us a factual update without inventing a legal conclusion or a response date.
  10. Regulatory affairs specialist: Exactly. We'll keep the filing record separate from whatever decision or acknowledgment follows.
A sensitive inquiry is not a validated instruction

Separate fictional case: a sales inbox receives a message labeled lawful intercept inquiry; the sender's authority and the request's applicability have not been verified.

  1. Sales operations lead: We received an email labeled lawful intercept inquiry. Should I tell the customer we'll comply immediately?
  2. Compliance liaison: No. The label doesn't establish the sender's authority, authenticity, or what action is applicable.
  3. Sales operations lead: I won't interpret the request. Which facts can I state when I escalate it?
  4. Compliance liaison: State that the email was received and that validation is pending. Use the designated restricted review channel.
  5. Sales operations lead: Should I forward the full message to the general account team so everyone is informed?
  6. Compliance liaison: Keep access limited through our established process. Don't spread potentially sensitive details unnecessarily.
  7. Sales operations lead: Understood. I can record receipt without describing the message as a verified legal demand.
  8. Compliance liaison: Exactly. The authorized team needs to assess it before anyone makes commitments or takes action.
  9. Sales operations lead: I'll route it for review and avoid customer-facing claims about our obligations or response timing.
  10. Compliance liaison: I'll confirm receipt by the responsible team while keeping the substantive outcome explicitly unresolved.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

Always works is not an approved notice
  1. Read turns 1-10; explain why the absolute claim conflicts with the known dependencies.
  2. Swap roles for turns 11-20; keep prominence, version, and review status explicit.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Track a notice through review

Draft N8 says a voice service never fails. Product records identify a power dependency. The local release process requires product and compliance approval. Neither team has approved N8.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Editor: "The unsupported absolute is ___."

Answer and explanation

C. never fails

Never fails promises an unconditional result that the supplied product facts do not establish.

2. Reviewer: "The identified technical condition is a ___."

Answer and explanation

D. power dependency

The product record explicitly identifies dependence on power as a condition to explain.

3. Editor: "The required reviewers are ___."

Answer and explanation

B. product and compliance

Both teams are named in the local release process and neither has approved the draft.

4. Reviewer: "The release status is ___."

Answer and explanation

D. not approved

The stated approvals are absent, so revision work alone cannot authorize release.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Lesson 06: Regulatory and Emergency Services
Original fictional case: A U.S. interconnected Voice over Internet Protocol provider is preparing a customer notice. The draft says, "911 always works, even without power or broadband." Product records identify dependence on powered customer equipment and a broadband connection, but the draft has not been reviewed against the current applicable requirements or approved service wording. Communications editor Celia and compliance reviewer Marcus must remove the absolute assurance, obtain verified service-specific limitations, and route the notice for review. The task is not to invent a universal disclaimer or conduct an emergency test call.
Language workshop: Always works is not an approved notice
Communication goal: Challenge an unsupported service claim and coordinate a clear, service-specific customer notice with the responsible reviewers.
Language guidance: VoIP describes a technology family; interconnected VoIP is a specific U.S. regulatory category. Emergency-calling obligations and location arrangements depend on the service and applicable rules. Do not turn one product example into a universal statement about all calling services.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): The draft says that 911 always works. / That assurance is not supported by the product record. / The service depends on powered equipment and broadband. / We are reviewing a U.S. interconnected-VoIP service. / Which current requirements apply to this specific service? / Please confirm the service-specific limitations. / We should not reuse a generic notice without checking applicability. / The customer needs to understand when calling may be limited or unavailable. / The limitation should not disappear into fine print. / A revised draft is not yet approved for publication. / Which document version has the reviewer cleared? / Please retain the review outcome with the approved text. / Can we substantiate always under the stated conditions? / We are not making an emergency call to test this wording. / Product and compliance need to verify the technical facts and applicable language. / Keep the notice on hold until the responsible review is complete.
Vocabulary: VoIP: Voice over Internet Protocol; voice communication carried over IP networks. / interconnected VoIP: A regulatory service category connecting IP-based voice with the public telephone network. / E911: Enhanced 911; emergency calling with associated routing and caller information functions. / PSAP: Public safety answering point; a facility receiving emergency calls. / dispatchable location: Location information sufficient to identify where assistance is needed under applicable rules. / registered location: Location information supplied or maintained for a service under applicable arrangements. / fixed service: A service associated with a fixed location under the relevant definition. / non-fixed service: A service not restricted to a fixed location under the relevant definition. / nomadic use: Use of a service from changing locations. / power dependency: Reliance on electrical power for service operation. / broadband dependency: Reliance on a broadband connection for service operation. / backup power: An alternative power source intended to support operation during a primary-power loss. / service limitation: A condition restricting availability, function, or performance. / customer notification: Information provided to customers about a relevant service matter. / affirmative acknowledgment: Active confirmation of specified information; this advisory context requires receipt and understanding. / prominence: The degree to which important information is noticeable. / plain-language notice: A notice using understandable wording while preserving necessary facts. / absolute assurance: An unconditional promise such as always or never. / applicability: Whether a requirement covers the specific service or situation. / approved wording: Language authorized through the responsible review process. / version control: Identification and management of changes to a document or record. / compliance review: Assessment against applicable requirements by responsible personnel. / publication hold: A restriction preventing release until specified conditions are met. / review record: Documentation of who reviewed which version and the result.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-10; explain why the absolute claim conflicts with the known dependencies. Swap roles for turns 11-20; keep prominence, version, and review status explicit. Complete the transfer as a notice review, without making an emergency test call.
Partner: Celia: Customer communications editor / Marcus: Compliance reviewer
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Draft N8 says a voice service never fails. Product records identify a power dependency. The local release process requires product and compliance approval. Neither team has approved N8.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Celia: I will ask product to verify the limitations and compliance to identify the current requirements, including the relevant customer-notification process.
Marcus: Yes. Retain the review record. Do not describe our edited paragraph as a complete compliance solution before those checks and any associated process requirements are addressed.
Celia: And we are reviewing language, not asking staff to call emergency services to see what happens.
Marcus: Correct. Once the responsible process is complete, use the approved wording for this service. Keep the customer consequence clear and do not restore an unsupported promise for a more reassuring tone.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

07Customer Churn and Service RecoveryA retention conversation without a false guarantee+

Your goal

Acknowledge repeated disruption, offer accountable follow-up, and distinguish service recovery from unsupported retention promises.

This lesson in the book, page 58 ↗

A Read the situation

A retention conversation without a false guarantee

Business customer Leila reports three broadband interruptions this week and is considering cancellation. The service is currently working, but the incident records do not yet establish a single shared cause. Account specialist Mateo can coordinate a technical review and provide a named update at 16:00 Central the next day. A service-credit request may be reviewed under the actual contract and applicable requirements; no amount is approved. Leila wants a guarantee that the problem will never recur. Mateo must respond honestly and preserve access to the cancellation process rather than making support conditional on staying.

Leila: Business customer / Mateo: Account specialist

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What has Leila reported?

Answer and explanation

C. Three broadband interruptions this week

Leila reports three interruptions, while the records have not established a shared technical cause.

2. What can Mateo commit to?

Answer and explanation

B. A coordinated review and an update at 16:00 Central the next day

Mateo can own the review and communication, but no permanent repair or compensation result is established.

3. What should happen if Leila wants to cancel?

Answer and explanation

D. Provide the applicable cancellation route without withholding support.

The brief requires preserving access to the actual cancellation process rather than using support as leverage.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Three interruptions establish a repeated customer experience, not necessarily one recurring technical fault. Say the records are being compared until a common cause is supported. Service currently working does not erase earlier disruption or establish that recurrence is impossible.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
churn
Customer loss over a defined period or the process of leaving a service.analyze customer churn
retention
Efforts to maintain an ongoing customer relationship.handle a retention conversation
service recovery
Actions taken to address a poor service experience.coordinate service recovery
repeat incident
A further incident involving a previously affected customer or service.review repeat incidents
recurrence
The return of a problem after an earlier occurrence.investigate recurrence
common cause
A cause shared by multiple events.establish a common cause
impact statement
A concise account of the customer's operational consequences.record the impact statement
case owner
The person accountable for coordinating a case.name the case owner
follow-up commitment
A specific promise about a later action or contact.record the follow-up commitment
service credit
A contract-related adjustment for eligible service performance issues.review a service-credit request
goodwill adjustment
A discretionary commercial adjustment offered under applicable authority.request a goodwill adjustment
eligibility
Whether the conditions for a process or benefit are met.assess credit eligibility
SLA
Service-level agreement; defined service commitments and associated terms.review the SLA
availability
The extent to which a service is usable during a defined measurement period.measure service availability
downtime
Time during which a defined service is unavailable.reconcile downtime records
exclusion
A circumstance left outside a specified contractual provision.check the exclusions
remediation plan
A planned set of actions to address an identified problem.confirm the remediation plan
cancellation request
A customer's request to end a service.record the cancellation request
notice period
The time required between a notice and its effective action under applicable terms.verify the notice period
termination charge
A charge that may apply when ending a contract under specified conditions.check the termination charge
complaint escalation
Referral of a complaint to a further review level.explain the complaint escalation route
relationship risk
The likelihood or consequence of damage to an ongoing customer relationship.assess relationship risk
service history
The record of service events and interactions over time.review the service history
reliability claim
A statement about a service's dependable operation.substantiate a reliability claim

A service credit and a goodwill adjustment can follow different rules and approval routes. Record the request accurately without promising eligibility or an amount. Explain the actual cancellation and complaint processes without making support conditional on retention.

C Notice the language

Acknowledge the cost without promising the impossible

A customer may need recognition of operational disruption before hearing the technical explanation. Name the repeated impact, then offer specific ownership and timing. Avoid using a sympathetic tone to smuggle in a promise about cause, compensation, or future reliability.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Acknowledge repetition
Three interruptions in one week have disrupted your team.
Recognize the consequence
I understand why you are questioning the service.
Separate present status
The connection is working now, but that does not resolve your concern.
Avoid a common-cause assumption
We have not yet established that all three incidents share one cause.
Decline the guarantee honestly
I cannot promise that an interruption will never happen again.
Offer ownership
I will coordinate the review and remain your contact.
Make the update specific
I will update you tomorrow at 16:00 Central.
Preserve the distinction
That is a progress update, not a guaranteed resolution time.
Capture the credit request
I will submit the service-credit request for review.
Qualify the outcome
Eligibility and any amount remain unconfirmed.
Separate commercial routes
A goodwill adjustment is not the same as an SLA credit.
Respect the customer's choice
I can explain the cancellation route while the review continues.
Avoid a condition
You do not need to withdraw the complaint to receive support.
Ask for evidence
Let us compare the incident times with the service history.
Explain escalation
I will provide the applicable complaint-review route.
Close with accountability
You will receive the review status, open questions, and next actions at the agreed time.
6 language and precision notes
Working now, but
Acknowledges current recovery without minimizing the prior impact.
Not yet established
Keeps a causal claim open until evidence supports it.
I cannot promise
Gives an honest limit instead of an impossible assurance.
I will coordinate
Offers a concrete action within the speaker's control.
For review
Records a request without granting its outcome.
While ... continues
Allows parallel support and customer-choice processes.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which response best handles never again?

Answer and explanation

D. I cannot guarantee that, but I will coordinate the review and update you tomorrow at 16:00 Central.

The response honestly limits the assurance while making a specific, accountable follow-up commitment.

2. Which credit statement is accurate?

Answer and explanation

C. I will submit the request; eligibility and amount remain unconfirmed.

No approved amount or eligibility decision is supplied, so the request must remain under review.

3. What should the technical summary say?

Answer and explanation

D. Three interruptions are reported; a shared cause is not yet established.

The summary distinguishes repeated impact from an unverified common-cause explanation.

4. How should cancellation be handled?

Answer and explanation

B. Explain the applicable route while continuing the review.

The customer should receive accurate process information without support being used as leverage.

E Conversations

A retention conversation without a false guarantee

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Leila: Business customer / Mateo: Account specialist

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • complaint escalation
  • goodwill adjustment
  • follow-up commitment
  • case owner
  • service history
  • common cause
  • reliability claim
  • cancellation request
  • service recovery
  • service credit
  1. Leila
    Three interruptions this week have meant rearranging customer calls again and again. I am considering moving. What will be different about this follow-up?
  2. Mateo
    I understand the 1 needs to be more than an apology. Three interruptions have affected your work, even though the connection is operating at the moment.
  3. Leila
    The last person said it was fixed. Why should I trust another explanation if the same thing keeps happening?
  4. Mateo
    We need to compare the 2 before calling it the same technical fault. The repeated impact is clear, but the records do not yet establish one common cause.
  5. Leila
    Please give me one contact who has all three records. Each time I call, I seem to be starting from the first interruption.
  6. Mateo
    I will be the 3 for the coordinated review. I will link the three incident records and make sure the technical team sees the times and the effect on your business.
  7. Leila
    Can you guarantee that it will never happen again? That is the assurance I need before deciding to stay.
  8. Mateo
    I cannot make that 4. I can promise a defined review and communication, but it would be misleading to guarantee that no interruption could ever occur.
  9. Leila
    Then tell me exactly when I will hear from you and what that contact will contain.
  10. Mateo
    My 5 is tomorrow at 16:00 Central. I will provide the review status, any verified findings, the questions still open, and the next actions.
  11. Leila
    We have lost time dealing with this. I also want the charges reviewed, not just the technical records.
  12. Mateo
    I will submit a 6 request under the actual agreement and applicable requirements. I cannot confirm eligibility or an amount before that review has taken place.
  13. Leila
    Someone mentioned a goodwill payment. Is that the same thing as a credit under the service agreement?
  14. Mateo
    A 7 can follow a different approval route. I will keep the requests distinct so that we do not imply one decision automatically resolves the other.
  15. Leila
    I still want the cancellation information. I do not want to be told I must stay before anyone investigates.
  16. Mateo
    I will provide the applicable 8 process while the review continues. You do not have to withdraw the complaint to receive support, and I will not invent terms to discourage your decision.
  17. Leila
    That is clearer. Please do not send a message saying the root cause is confirmed unless the technical team can support it.
  18. Mateo
    Agreed. We will describe a 9 only if the evidence connects the events. If the review is still incomplete at the update time, I will say that and explain what remains.
  19. Leila
    I will expect your update tomorrow at four Central, with the technical status and the separate credit-review status.
  20. Mateo
    Yes, and I will include the 10 route if you want that review. My update commitment stands whether you decide to retain the service or proceed with cancellation.

Answers and explanations
  1. service recovery Turn 2. Service recovery addresses the poor experience through concrete follow-up, not just an attempt to retain the account.
  2. service history Turn 4. Service history supplies the incident and interaction record needed to compare events before claiming they share the same cause.
  3. case owner Turn 6. Case owner identifies the person coordinating the review and communication, giving the customer a clear point of accountability.
  4. reliability claim Turn 8. Reliability claim concerns dependable service; the current evidence cannot support an unconditional no-recurrence guarantee.
  5. follow-up commitment Turn 10. Follow-up commitment specifies a controlled action and time, not a guaranteed repair deadline.
  6. service credit Turn 12. Service credit is a possible contract-related adjustment that requires review; the conversation does not establish eligibility or an approved amount.
  7. goodwill adjustment Turn 14. Goodwill adjustment is a discretionary commercial route, which may have different conditions and authority from an SLA-related credit.
  8. cancellation request Turn 16. Cancellation request concerns ending service; access to that process must not depend on withdrawing the complaint.
  9. common cause Turn 18. Common cause means an explanation shared by the incidents; repeated customer impact alone does not establish that technical relationship.
  10. complaint escalation Turn 20. Complaint escalation provides a further review route for unresolved concerns, distinct from technical work, compensation review, and cancellation.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Leila: Three interruptions this week have meant rearranging customer calls again and again. I am considering moving. What will be different about this follow-up?
  2. Mateo: I understand the service recovery needs to be more than an apology. Three interruptions have affected your work, even though the connection is operating at the moment.
  3. Leila: The last person said it was fixed. Why should I trust another explanation if the same thing keeps happening?
  4. Mateo: We need to compare the service history before calling it the same technical fault. The repeated impact is clear, but the records do not yet establish one common cause.
  5. Leila: Please give me one contact who has all three records. Each time I call, I seem to be starting from the first interruption.
  6. Mateo: I will be the case owner for the coordinated review. I will link the three incident records and make sure the technical team sees the times and the effect on your business.
  7. Leila: Can you guarantee that it will never happen again? That is the assurance I need before deciding to stay.
  8. Mateo: I cannot make that reliability claim. I can promise a defined review and communication, but it would be misleading to guarantee that no interruption could ever occur.
  9. Leila: Then tell me exactly when I will hear from you and what that contact will contain.
  10. Mateo: My follow-up commitment is tomorrow at 16:00 Central. I will provide the review status, any verified findings, the questions still open, and the next actions.
  11. Leila: We have lost time dealing with this. I also want the charges reviewed, not just the technical records.
  12. Mateo: I will submit a service credit request under the actual agreement and applicable requirements. I cannot confirm eligibility or an amount before that review has taken place.
  13. Leila: Someone mentioned a goodwill payment. Is that the same thing as a credit under the service agreement?
  14. Mateo: A goodwill adjustment can follow a different approval route. I will keep the requests distinct so that we do not imply one decision automatically resolves the other.
  15. Leila: I still want the cancellation information. I do not want to be told I must stay before anyone investigates.
  16. Mateo: I will provide the applicable cancellation request process while the review continues. You do not have to withdraw the complaint to receive support, and I will not invent terms to discourage your decision.
  17. Leila: That is clearer. Please do not send a message saying the root cause is confirmed unless the technical team can support it.
  18. Mateo: Agreed. We will describe a common cause only if the evidence connects the events. If the review is still incomplete at the update time, I will say that and explain what remains.
  19. Leila: I will expect your update tomorrow at four Central, with the technical status and the separate credit-review status.
  20. Mateo: Yes, and I will include the complaint escalation route if you want that review. My update commitment stands whether you decide to retain the service or proceed with cancellation.
Dialogue in the book, page 64 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Correcting a premature root-cause message

The lesson's customer asks about repeated service interruptions; the investigation has no confirmed root cause.

  1. Service assurance manager: I need to correct our earlier message. We described a cause before the investigation had confirmed it.
  2. Customer IT manager: That made us think the repeated interruptions were understood. What can you actually tell us now?
  3. Service assurance manager: The investigation remains open, and the root cause is not confirmed. I'm sorry our wording suggested otherwise.
  4. Customer IT manager: Can you at least promise the interruptions won't happen again after the next check?
  5. Service assurance manager: I can't make that promise from the current evidence. I can give you verified findings as they emerge.
  6. Customer IT manager: Please separate the investigation from any discussion of an SLA credit. We need clarity on both.
  7. Service assurance manager: Agreed. Credit eligibility needs the applicable service agreement and review; it isn't established by this conversation.
  8. Customer IT manager: A consistent contact would help. We keep having to explain the same history to different people.
  9. Service assurance manager: I'll coordinate the service assurance updates and confirm the contact arrangement through our account team.
  10. Customer IT manager: Thank you. Accurate updates will be more useful than another confident explanation that later changes.
An update time was mistaken for a repair time

Separate fictional case: a support email promised an update at three; its subject line said service restored at three, but no restoration time is confirmed.

  1. Account service lead: Our subject line was wrong: three o'clock is the next update time, not a confirmed repair time.
  2. Customer operations manager: We arranged work around that subject line. The correction changes what our staff can plan.
  3. Account service lead: I understand, and I'm sorry for the misleading wording. Restoration timing is still unconfirmed.
  4. Customer operations manager: Will your next message actually contain a fix, or might it just report that investigation continues?
  5. Account service lead: It may report continued investigation. We'll distinguish the update commitment from the unresolved service estimate.
  6. Customer operations manager: Please correct the original message in the same channel so our branch managers see it.
  7. Account service lead: I will, and I'll place the correction first rather than bury it beneath technical detail.
  8. Customer operations manager: Could you also make the time zone explicit? Our teams work in different regions.
  9. Account service lead: Yes. I'll use the agreed update time with its time zone and keep restoration status separate.
  10. Customer operations manager: That gives us a reliable communication plan even while the service investigation remains open.
A ticket closure did not resolve the complaint

Separate fictional case: a service ticket was administratively closed while the customer's recurring dropouts remained under a separate investigation; the closure message implied resolution.

  1. Customer care supervisor: Our closure message suggested the dropouts were resolved. That was inaccurate; the separate investigation remains open.
  2. Customer IT lead: Then why did we receive a message asking us to confirm that everything was fixed?
  3. Customer care supervisor: The message reflected the ticket workflow, not verified service recovery. We should have explained that distinction.
  4. Customer IT lead: Please don't make us restart the complaint just to keep the recurring fault visible.
  5. Customer care supervisor: I'll connect your complaint to the continuing investigation through our service assurance process and confirm the reference.
  6. Customer IT lead: Does that mean you know the root cause now, or only that someone is still investigating?
  7. Customer care supervisor: Only that the investigation continues. I won't imply that linking records establishes the cause.
  8. Customer IT lead: I need a clear owner and accurate status, not another generic resolved notification.
  9. Customer care supervisor: Understood. I'll correct the message and confirm ownership without promising an unverified repair or credit.
  10. Customer IT lead: Please do. That will help restore confidence that our actual service problem is being followed.

Answer and explanation

C. two

The record supplies two incidents, without establishing that they share a cause.

Answer and explanation

C. Sol

Sol is the agent explicitly assigned responsibility for coordinating this review.

3. Agent: "The next update is ___."

Answer and explanation

D. Tuesday at 11:00 Mountain

The commitment specifies a day, time, and time zone for communication.

4. Customer: "The credit outcome remains ___."

Answer and explanation

D. undecided

No eligibility or amount decision has been made for the service-credit request.

Lesson 07: Customer Churn and Service Recovery
Original fictional case: Business customer Leila reports three broadband interruptions this week and is considering cancellation. The service is currently working, but the incident records do not yet establish a single shared cause. Account specialist Mateo can coordinate a technical review and provide a named update at 16:00 Central the next day. A service-credit request may be reviewed under the actual contract and applicable requirements; no amount is approved. Leila wants a guarantee that the problem will never recur. Mateo must respond honestly and preserve access to the cancellation process rather than making support conditional on staying.
Language workshop: A retention conversation without a false guarantee
Communication goal: Acknowledge repeated disruption, offer accountable follow-up, and distinguish service recovery from unsupported retention promises.
Language guidance: Three interruptions establish a repeated customer experience, not necessarily one recurring technical fault. Say the records are being compared until a common cause is supported. Service currently working does not erase earlier disruption or establish that recurrence is impossible.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Three interruptions in one week have disrupted your team. / I understand why you are questioning the service. / The connection is working now, but that does not resolve your concern. / We have not yet established that all three incidents share one cause. / I cannot promise that an interruption will never happen again. / I will coordinate the review and remain your contact. / I will update you tomorrow at 16:00 Central. / That is a progress update, not a guaranteed resolution time. / I will submit the service-credit request for review. / Eligibility and any amount remain unconfirmed. / A goodwill adjustment is not the same as an SLA credit. / I can explain the cancellation route while the review continues. / You do not need to withdraw the complaint to receive support. / Let us compare the incident times with the service history. / I will provide the applicable complaint-review route. / You will receive the review status, open questions, and next actions at the agreed time.
Vocabulary: churn: Customer loss over a defined period or the process of leaving a service. / retention: Efforts to maintain an ongoing customer relationship. / service recovery: Actions taken to address a poor service experience. / repeat incident: A further incident involving a previously affected customer or service. / recurrence: The return of a problem after an earlier occurrence. / common cause: A cause shared by multiple events. / impact statement: A concise account of the customer's operational consequences. / case owner: The person accountable for coordinating a case. / follow-up commitment: A specific promise about a later action or contact. / service credit: A contract-related adjustment for eligible service performance issues. / goodwill adjustment: A discretionary commercial adjustment offered under applicable authority. / eligibility: Whether the conditions for a process or benefit are met. / SLA: Service-level agreement; defined service commitments and associated terms. / availability: The extent to which a service is usable during a defined measurement period. / downtime: Time during which a defined service is unavailable. / exclusion: A circumstance left outside a specified contractual provision. / remediation plan: A planned set of actions to address an identified problem. / cancellation request: A customer's request to end a service. / notice period: The time required between a notice and its effective action under applicable terms. / termination charge: A charge that may apply when ending a contract under specified conditions. / complaint escalation: Referral of a complaint to a further review level. / relationship risk: The likelihood or consequence of damage to an ongoing customer relationship. / service history: The record of service events and interactions over time. / reliability claim: A statement about a service's dependable operation.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-10; acknowledge the repeated impact and give a bounded commitment. Swap roles for turns 11-20; separate technical, credit, and cancellation processes. Complete the transfer with the named owner and the exact update time.
Partner: Leila: Business customer / Mateo: Account specialist
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Customer Nera reports two interruptions. Agent Sol owns the review and promises an update on Tuesday at 11:00 Mountain. The shared cause and any service credit remain undecided.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Leila: That is clearer. Please do not send a message saying the root cause is confirmed unless the technical team can support it.
Mateo: Agreed. We will describe a common cause only if the evidence connects the events. If the review is still incomplete at the update time, I will say that and explain what remains.
Leila: I will expect your update tomorrow at four Central, with the technical status and the separate credit-review status.
Mateo: Yes, and I will include the complaint escalation route if you want that review. My update commitment stands whether you decide to retain the service or proceed with cancellation.
END REFERENCE

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning

3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.
