Use the occupation's natural nomenclature and register. Avoid an interview between a teacher and a learner, generic small talk, and inserting a glossary definition into every reply. Explain specialist terms outside the dialogue instead.
4. After the script, explain five useful expressions in context and identify two grammar or register choices. Give three four-choice questions about the speakers' reasoning, with exactly one answer supported by the dialogue. Wait for my selections, then explain why each answer fits and why its alternatives fail. Add a role-switch reading using the supplied script; do not ask for an essay or invented written reply.
5. Before presenting a script, check names, numbers, chronology, roles and terminology for consistency. Label invented facts and acknowledge uncertain specialist usage. If I requested all six, deliver one complete script at a time and wait for "next". Make each scenario materially different.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

Course: Telecommunications English
Professional audience: network operations staff, telecom engineers, field technicians, customer operations teams, product managers, regulatory staff, and telecom project leaders

Regulatory examples identify their jurisdiction and are not complete compliance instructions.
Dialogue 04: Provisioning mismatch
Original fictional setting: A service activation fails despite a completed order status.
Professional roles: Provisioning specialist / Service delivery manager
Published dialogue (reference script, not my own performance):
Provisioning specialist: The order is marked complete, but the customer's circuit isn't passing the acceptance test.
Service delivery manager: Which stage failed: configuration, physical connectivity, or test validation?
Provisioning specialist: The port configuration doesn't match the approved service profile.
Service delivery manager: Route the correction through the authorized change process and retain the test results.
Provisioning specialist: I'll verify the configuration and repeat the agreed acceptance checks after the change.
Service delivery manager: I'll keep customer acceptance pending and explain the next confirmed step to the account team.
Vocabulary in this exchange: circuit: A telecommunications connection or path used to carry a service.
END REFERENCE
Dialogue 05: Field dispatch handoff
Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Dialogue 05: Field dispatch handoff
Original fictional setting: A technician receives an incomplete fault ticket.
Professional roles: Field technician / Dispatch coordinator
Published dialogue (reference script, not my own performance):
Field technician: I have the site address, but no access contact or equipment reference.
Dispatch coordinator: I'll obtain both before confirming the visit. The ticket currently lists only the customer's billing address.
Field technician: Please also confirm whether any access restrictions or hazards are recorded.
Dispatch coordinator: I'll check the work order and required site arrangements.
Field technician: Once those are complete, I can assess the tools and skills needed for the job.
Dispatch coordinator: I'll update the ticket and send you the verified work location before dispatch.
Vocabulary in this exchange: dispatch: Coordinating and assigning resources for an operational task.
END REFERENCE

Dialogue 06: Emergency-service change review
Original fictional setting: A network change has potential emergency-service implications.
Professional roles: Change manager / Compliance specialist
Published dialogue (reference script, not my own performance):
Change manager: This routing change affects a service used for emergency calling. Can it follow the ordinary release schedule?
Compliance specialist: It needs the applicable specialist review, testing, and coordination before approval.
Change manager: The current change record doesn't identify that dependency.
Compliance specialist: Add the affected services and ask the responsible engineering team to define the required evidence.
Change manager: I'll keep implementation pending and update the review group.
Compliance specialist: Record the approval conditions clearly; a successful general connectivity test isn't enough to close this issue.
END REFERENCE

Dialogue 07: Churn-risk discussion
Original fictional setting: A service manager responds to a customer's repeated interruptions.
Professional roles: Account manager / Service assurance lead
Published dialogue (reference script, not my own performance):
Account manager: The customer is considering leaving after three outages. Can we promise there won't be another?
Service assurance lead: No. We can explain the confirmed incidents, completed improvements, and remaining work accurately.
Account manager: They also want a service-credit review.
Service assurance lead: Route that against the actual agreement and incident records; don't promise an amount before review.
Account manager: I'll arrange a meeting around the recovery actions and communication plan.
Service assurance lead: I'll bring the evidence and identify who owns each outstanding reliability action.
END REFERENCE

Dialogue 08: Equipment end of support
Original fictional setting: A network team plans replacement of aging equipment.
Professional roles: Network planner / Vendor manager
Published dialogue (reference script, not my own performance):
Network planner: The router family reaches end of support next year. Does that mean it stops operating then?
Vendor manager: No, but the vendor's support and update arrangements change. We need to verify the exact notice and dates.
Network planner: I'll map installed units and service dependencies.
Vendor manager: I'll confirm replacement options, lead times, and contractual support conditions.
Network planner: Then we can prioritize migration by exposure and business impact.
Vendor manager: Include test, rollback, and maintenance-window requirements in the plan before committing customer dates.
Vocabulary in this exchange: end of support: The point after which a supplier stops providing specified support for a product.
END REFERENCE
Create more scenario dialogues — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Extend the conversation library
1. Propose six distinct, common scenarios in this field beyond the supplied case or script. For each, name the setting, two or more professional roles, the communication problem and one useful terminology focus. Include routine coordination, clarification, a complication, disagreement, handoff and follow-up where relevant. Choose scenarios that fit this profession, rather than forcing unsuitable situations into it.
2. Ask me to choose one scenario, or request all six in sequence. Stop and wait. Do not write all the scripts before I choose.
3. For the selected scenario, write an original fictional dialogue of 12-18 substantial speaking turns between two or three professionals. Name each role, establish an actual work problem, and let the exchange progress through questions, clarification, competing constraints and a credible next step or explicitly unresolved issue. Use the occupation's natural nomenclature and register. Avoid an interview between a teacher and a learner, generic small talk, and inserting a glossary definition into every reply. Explain specialist terms outside the dialogue instead.
4. After the script, explain five useful expressions in context and identify two grammar or register choices. Give three four-choice questions about the speakers' reasoning, with exactly one answer supported by the dialogue. Wait for my selections, then explain why each answer fits and why its alternatives fail. Add a role-switch reading using the supplied script; do not ask for an essay or invented written reply.
5. Before presenting a script, check names, numbers, chronology, roles and terminology for consistency. Label invented facts and acknowledge uncertain specialist usage. If I requested all six, deliver one complete script at a time and wait for "next". Make each scenario materially different.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

REFERENCE
Course: Telecommunications English
Professional audience: network operations staff, telecom engineers, field technicians, customer operations teams, product managers, regulatory staff, and telecom project leaders
Scope: All operators, customers, circuits, incidents, dates, prices, contracts, and local procedures are fictional. This book teaches professional English, not network configuration, field-safety procedures, legal compliance, or emergency-call handling. Use approved operating procedures and qualified personnel. Regulatory examples identify their jurisdiction and are not complete compliance instructions.
Dialogue 08: Equipment end of support
Original fictional setting: A network team plans replacement of aging equipment.
Professional roles: Network planner / Vendor manager
Published dialogue (reference script, not my own performance):
Network planner: The router family reaches end of support next year. Does that mean it stops operating then?
Vendor manager: No, but the vendor's support and update arrangements change. We need to verify the exact notice and dates.
Network planner: I'll map installed units and service dependencies.
Vendor manager: I'll confirm replacement options, lead times, and contractual support conditions.
Network planner: Then we can prioritize migration by exposure and business impact.
Vendor manager: Include test, rollback, and maintenance-window requirements in the plan before committing customer dates.
Vocabulary in this exchange: end of support: The point after which a supplier stops providing specified support for a product.
END REFERENCE

REFERENCE
Course: Telecommunications English
Professional audience: network operations staff, telecom engineers, field technicians, customer operations teams, product managers, regulatory staff, and telecom project leaders
Scope: All operators, customers, circuits, incidents, dates, prices, contracts, and local procedures are fictional. This book teaches professional English, not network configuration, field-safety procedures, legal compliance, or emergency-call handling. Use approved operating procedures and qualified personnel. Regulatory examples identify their jurisdiction and are not complete compliance instructions.
Lesson 01: Network Operations and Outage Bridges
Original fictional case: At 09:10, incident O73 interrupts 120 broadband services in the East service area. The number is a count of services, not individual people. Network alarms confirm the interruption, but the cause has not been isolated. No estimated time of restoration has been verified. Network operations lead Arun and customer communications lead Tess join an outage bridge. Their next public update is due at 09:40 Eastern. They need a consistent message without inventing a restoration time or blaming a recent change before evidence connects it to the incident.
Language workshop: The next update is not a restoration promise
Communication goal: Give a concise incident update that separates verified impact, investigation, and a timed communication commitment.
Language guidance: An affected-service count is not automatically a count of customers, households, or people. Retain the measurement unit in every update. A shared incident identifier helps different teams discuss the same event without merging unrelated reports.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Here is the confirmed service impact. / The interruption is limited to the East service area in our current evidence. / We have identified 120 affected broadband services. / The interruption is confirmed; the cause is still under investigation. / The recent change is a lead, not a confirmed cause. / We do not yet have a verified restoration estimate. / The next public update is at 09:40 Eastern. / That is an update time, not a restoration commitment. / Which monitoring result supports that statement? / Has the proposed workaround been approved and validated? / Let us separate current impact from the investigation hypotheses. / Arun will confirm the technical status before the message goes out. / Please add that event and its time to the incident timeline. / We need the agreed monitoring evidence before closing the incident. / Customer care and the status page should use the same verified facts. / I will circulate the impact, open questions, owner, and next update time.
Vocabulary: NOC: Network operations center; the team or facility monitoring network service. / outage bridge: A coordinated call used to manage an interruption. / incident commander: The person coordinating the incident response. / service impact: The effect of an incident on a defined service. / blast radius: Informal term for the extent of an incident's effects. / fault isolation: Investigation to locate the source of a fault. / alarm correlation: Analysis of related alerts to find a common pattern. / root cause: The underlying cause established through investigation. / ETR: Estimated time of restoration; a forecast, not automatically a guarantee. / restoration: Return of the affected service to its required operating state. / degradation: Reduced performance without necessarily losing the entire service. / packet loss: Failure of some transmitted data packets to reach their destination. / latency: Time taken for data to travel through a specified path. / jitter: Variation in packet delay over time. / failover: Transfer to an alternative component or path after a problem. / redundancy: Additional capacity or components intended to improve resilience. / workaround: A temporary way to reduce impact without resolving the underlying cause. / change freeze: A restriction on changes during a defined period. / rollback: Return to a prior configuration or release under an approved process. / escalation path: The defined route for involving additional authority or expertise. / status cadence: The agreed frequency of updates. / incident timeline: A chronological record of relevant incident events. / monitoring window: A defined period for observing service behavior. / post-incident review: A structured review of the response and underlying learning.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-10; keep the service count and update time separate from people and restoration. Swap roles for turns 11-20; request the evidence needed before announcing recovery. Complete the transfer and retain its service type, area, and time zone.
Partner: Arun: Network operations lead / Tess: Customer communications lead
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Incident R16 affects 45 voice services in the North area from 14:05. The next update is at 14:30 Central. The cause and restoration estimate remain unverified.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Tess: Then the next message may still say we are investigating, but it will have a named owner and a clear time.
Arun: Yes. If the impact changes, describe the restoration status precisely. Do not replace an unknown cause with a guess just to make the message feel more complete.
Tess: I will keep the 120-service figure, the East area, the investigation status, and the 09:40 Eastern update commitment together.
Arun: Good. Use that same factual core across channels. After recovery, the post-incident review can address the fuller explanation and any follow-up actions.
END REFERENCE

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Paste into a new chat, then follow the exercise one step at a time. English Ladder does not send anything to an AI service. Your chosen service's terms and any usage charges apply.

AI responses can contain mistakes. Compare feedback with the lesson and check specialist claims against the course references. Use these exercises for language practice; follow qualified guidance for actual professional decisions.

For teachers and study partners

Ask learners to respond before revealing the model. Give feedback on one meaning issue and one language pattern, then let them repeat. For mixed levels, offer the frames first and remove them in the second round.
